8. [Jean Claude, An Historical Defence of the Reformation: In Answer to a Book Intituled, Just Prejudices against the Calvinists. Translated into English by T. B. (London, 1683). A Huguenot writer, Claude first published his Defence in 1673.] ↩
9. [A Collection of Confessions of Faith, Catechisms, Directories, Books of Disciplines, &c. (2 vols., Edinburgh, 1719—22), 2, 123.] ↩
1. [Andrew Gray, Eleven Communion Sermons, Preached at Several Places, with Speeches and Exhortations at the Tables (Edinburgh, 1716); the second of these titles has not been located. Gray (1633—56) was a minister in the Church of Scotland.] ↩
2. [Ebenezer Erskine, The Stone rejected by the Builders, exalted as the Head-Stone of the Corner. A Sermon Preach'd At the Opening of the Synod of Perth and Stirling (Edinburgh, 1732). A Seceder, in 1733 Erskine (1680—1754) left the Church of Scotland over issues of church and state.] ↩
3. [Ralph Erskine, Christ The People's Covenant. A Sermon Preached immediately before the Celebration of the Lord's Supper (1725; 4th ed., Glasgow, 1739); and Erskine, Christ's Treasures opened by himself, declaring he hath all Things that God the Father hath (Glasgow, 1747), pp. 39—41. Brother of Ebenezer, Ralph Erskine (1685—1752) also became a Seceder.] ↩
4. [John Willison, The Church's Danger, and the Minister's Duty Declared, in a Sermon Preach'd at the Opening of the Synod of Angus and Mearns (Edinburgh, 1733), pp. 47—51. Willison (1680—1750), was a leading member of the evangelical wing of the Church of Scotland, sometime friend and correspondent of the Erskines, correspondent of JE, and "the consummate Scottish catechist and spiritual writer." Leigh Eric Schmidt, Holy Fairs: Scottish Communions and American Revivals in the Early Modern Period (Princeton, Princeton Univ. Press, 1989), pp. 46, 232, n. 67.] ↩
5. [{John Willison}, A Defence of national churches; and particularly of the national constitution of the Church of Scotland… against the cavils of Independents (Edinburgh, 1719); no copy located.] ↩
6. [Thomas Ayton, A Review of Mr. Glass's Observes on the Original Constitution of the Christian Church (Edinburgh, 1731), p. 70. Ayton (1694—1739), minister in the Church of Scotland, defender of Presbyterianism against Independents, and author of The Original Constitution of the Christian Church (Edinburgh, 1730), which was criticized by John Glas (1695—1773), a deposed (as of 1730) minister in the Church of Scotland; JE cites Glas' critique in Misrepresentations Corrected; see below p. 363, n. 3.] ↩
7. [Ralph Naismith, A Treatise upon the Entail of the Covenant of Grace (Glasgow, 1725).] ↩
8. [John Warden, A Practical Essay on the Sacrament of Baptism; wherein The Doctrine of that Divine Ordinance is opened and explained; [and] the Constitution and Practice of the established Church of Scotland vindicated (Edinburgh, 1724). Warden (1671—1751) was a minister in the Church of Scotland and an advocate of mediating politics; in this treatise he relied on the concept of "fœderal Holiness" (p. 83).] ↩
9. [John Currie, A Sermon Preached in the Church of Kirkaldie, At the Opening of the Synod of Fife (Edinburgh, 1733), pp. 30—31. Currie (1699—1765) was a minister in the Church of Scotland and, although not a Seceder, a "friend and correspondent of Ebenezer and Ralph Erskine." Scott, Fasti Ecclesiæ 5, 97.] ↩
1. [John Anderson, A Defence of the Church-Government, Faith, Worship, & Spirit of the Presbyterians. In Answer to a late Book Intituled, An Apology for Mr. Thomas Rhind (Glasgow, 1714), p. 282. Anderson (1668?— 1721) was a minister in the Church of Scotland.] ↩
2. [The Confession of Faith, Together with The Larger and Lesser Catechismes, composed by the Reverend Assembly of Divines Sitting at Westminster (London, 1658), 1st pagination, p. 100; 2d pag., pp. 133—35.] ↩
3. [Samuel Bolton, The Guard of the Tree of Life: or, A Sacramentall Discourse; Shewing a Christians Priviledge… Duty… Danger (London, 1644), pp. 108—11. Bolton (1606—54) was a moderate within English Puritanism.] ↩
4. [Richard Baxter, A Petition for Peace: with the Reformation of the Liturgy. As it was Presented to the Right Reverend Bishops, by the Divines Appointed by His Majesties Commission (London, 1661); and Baxter, Christian Concord: containing, I. A Disswasive from unnecessary Division and Separation… II. The terms necessary for concord among all true Churches and Christians (London, Thomas Pankhurst, 1691). The first of these books was occasioned by the short-lived possibility that in 1660 the newly restored bishops would endorse a modified Book of Common Prayer acceptable to moderate Puritans like Baxter who wished to remain in the Church of England.] ↩
5. [Stephen Charnock, A Discourse Of the Subjects of the Lords Supper, in The Works of the late Learned Divine Stephen Charnock, B. D. (2 vols., London, Thomas Cokerill, 1684), 2, 776—813.] ↩
6. [Anthony Palmer, A Scripture-rale to the Lords Table (London, 1654), p. 11. Palmer (1618?— 97) was an Independent and Nonconformist.] ↩
7. [Humphrey Saunders, An Anti-Diatribe: or the Apologie of Some Ministers and godly people, asserting the lawfulnesse of their adminstring the Lords Supper in a select company (London, 1655). Saunders was criticizing John Humfrey.] ↩
8. [Samuel Langley, Suspension Reviewed, Stated, Cleered and Setled upon plain Scripture-Proof (London, B. Hayes for Thomas Underhill, 1658).] ↩
9. [Thomas Doolittle, A Treatise concerning the Lords Supper (12th ed., London, 1681). Doolittle (1632—1707) was a Presbyterian Nonconformist whose Treatise went through some twenty-six editions and was reprinted in Boston.] ↩
1. [Matthew Henry, The Communicant's Companion: Or, Instructions and Helps For the Right Receiving of the Lords Supper (London, Thomas Parkhurst, 1711). Henry (1662—1714) was a Presbyterian Nonconformist and contributor to the sacramental renaissance.] ↩
2. [Jabez Earle, Sacramental Exercises; or, the Christian's Employment before, at, and after the Lord's Supper (2d ed., London, 1718). Earle (1676?— 1768) was a Nonconformist and Presbyterian.] ↩
3. [John Shower, Sacramental Discourses, or several Texts, before, and after the Lords Supper (London, 1693). Shower (1657—1715) was a Nonconformist and Presbyterian.] ↩
4. [John Flavel, Gospel-Unity recommended to the Churches of Christ, in The Whole Works of the Reverend John Flavel (2 vols., London, 1716), 1, 679—84. Flavel (1630?— 91), a Nonconformist and Presbyterian, was a prolific and popular writer.] ↩
5. [{Matthew Henry}, An Account of the Life and Death of Mr. Philip Henry, Minister of the Gospel (3d ed., London, 1712). Philip (1631—96), father of Matthew, was an ejected minister and a Presbyterian.] ↩
6. [Isaac Gilling, The Life Of the Reverend Mr. George Trosse, Late Minister of the Gospel in Exon (London, 1715). Trosse (1631—1713) was a Presbyterian Nonconformist.] ↩
7. [Edward Calamy, A Practical Discourse concerning Vows; with special reference to Baptism and the Lords Supper (London, 1697); and Calamy, A Defence of Moderate Non-conformity (3 vols., London, 1703—05). Calamy (1671—1732), son and grandson of ministers of the same name, was a Nonconformist noted for defending "liberty of conscience."] ↩
8. [Matthew Henry, An Exposition of the Several Epistles Contained in the New Testament (6 vols., London, 1721), 6, 79—133. The sixth volume in this series was completed by other hands after Henry's death. The copy in Dr. Williams' Library, London, indicates that Simon Browne had written the commentary on I Corinthians. Browne (1680—1732) was a Nonconformist minister and a nonsubscriber in the Salter's Hall dispute of 1719] ↩
9. [William Harris, Self-Dedication, Personal and Sacramental; Briefly Explained and Enforced (London, 1717). Harris (1675—1740) was a Nonconformist, nonsubscriber, and early liberal.] ↩
1. [David Jennings, Sermons upon various subjects, preached to young people on New Years Day (London, T. Cox, 1730). Jennings (1691—1732) was an Independent and nonsubscriber in 1719.] ↩
2. [Daniel Williams, Gospel-truth Stated and Vindicated: Wherein some of Dr. Crisp's Opinions Are Considered; and the Opposite Truths Are Plainly Stated and Confirmed (London, 1692), p. 65 Williams (1643—1716), a moderate Calvinist, Presbyterian Nonconformist and ecclesiastical politician, responded to the posthumous printing (1690) of the Works of the mid-seventeenth-century Calvinist Tobias Crisp by denouncing him as a neonomian.] ↩
3. [John Guyse, A Sermon Preached at the Ordination Of the Reverend Thomas Gibbons… Together with an… Exhortation to Him, by Thomas Hall (London, 1743), pp. 49—50, 84—86. Guyse (1680—1761) was an Independent Nonconformist and co-author, with Isaac Watts, of the preface to the London printing of JE's Faithful Narrative.] ↩
4. [Isaac Watts, An Humble Attempt towards the Revival of Practical Religion among Christians (2d ed., London, 1739), pp. 179—80; and Watts, The Holiness of Times, Places, and People under the Jewish and Christian Dispensations Consider d and Compared, in Several Discourses (London, 1738), p. 194. Watts (1674—1748), Nonconformist minister, famous hymnist, liberal Calvinist, and a nonsubscriber in the Salter's Hall dispute, endorsed and arranged for the London Publication of the Faithful Narrative.] ↩
5. [Philip Doddridge, The Family Expositor; or, a Paraphrase and Version of the New Testament (6 vols., London, 1739—56), 3, 66, 119; 2, 421, 438.] ↩
6. [Stoddard, Appeal, p. 28.] ↩
7. [Stoddard, Doctrine of Instituted Churches, pp. 6, 17, 19.] ↩
8. [Stephen Lob, The True Dissenter, or, the Cause of those that are for Gathered Churches (London, 1685), pp. 97—99. Lob (d. 1699), an Independent Nonconformist, was a critic of Daniel Williams and Richard Baxter during the Crispian, or Neonomian, dispute in the 1690s.] ↩
9. [Baxter, Certain Disputations, pp. sig. C2 v., 417, 424. The several persons cited in this statement were controversialists in the mid-seventeenth century.] ↩
1. [Langley, Suspension Reviewed, pp. 121—22.] ↩
2. [{Jonathan Mitchel}, "An Answer to the Apologetical Preface," in {Richard Mather}, A Defence of the Answer and Arguments of the Synod met at Boston in the year 1662… Against a Reply made thereto by the Rev. Mr. J. Davenport (Cambridge, 1664). On Mitchel, see above pp. 27—28.] ↩
3. [Mitchel, "Propositions," in Mather, Magnalia, bk. 4, pp. 180, 161—62.] ↩
4. [{Mitchel}, "Answer," p. 46.] ↩
5. [Mitchel, "Propositions," in Mather, Magnalia, bk. 4, p. 178.] ↩
6. [{Mitchel}, "Answer," pp. 23—24.] ↩
7. [Eadem est ratio utriusque sacramenti: "In the same manner are reckoned both sacraments."] ↩
8. [Synopsis Purioris Theologiæ Disputationibus quinquaginta duabus comprehensa, … S. S. Theologiæ Doctores & Professores in Academia Leidensi (Lugduni, Batavorum, Elzeviriana, 1632).] ↩
9. [London, 1691.] ↩
1. [Lob, True Dissenter, pp. 96—97.] ↩
2. [Harris, Self-Dedication, pp. 115, 164.] ↩
3. [Watts, Holiness of Times, pp. 195—96, 210.] ↩
4. [Doddridge, Family Expositor, 6, pp. 132, 129.] ↩
5. [Anderson, Defence of the Church-Government, pp. 276—78.] ↩
6. [See above p. 331, n. 8.] ↩
7. [See above p. 331, n. 5.] ↩
8. [Baxter, Certain Disputations, pp. 195—96, 124, sig. E2 r., 197—205, 232, 204—05.] ↩
9. [Ibid., pp. 202, 53, 69, 96, 99, 181.] ↩
1. [Baxter, Petition for Peace, p. 58.] ↩
2. [Charnock, Works, 2, 502.] ↩
3. [Watts, Holiness of Times, pp. 189—90.] ↩
4. [Propositions Concerning the Subject of Baptism and Consociation of Churches, p. 24.] ↩
